February 21, 1940.
Ren
The data contained in this let-
ter are not easily susceptible of
summarization, but, as far as the
statistics are concerned, a summary
table will be found on page 5. You
will note from that table that from
1936 to193, inclusive, the Japa-
nese share of imports of kerosene
into north China (Yangtze valley is
included) increased from 5 to 19%;
the Japanese share of imports of
gasoline increased from 2 to 10%;
the Japanese share of imports of
lubricating oil increased from 10
to 36%; and the Japanese share of
imports of Diesel oil increased
from 1 to 8%. These figures relate
to commercial shipments and do not
include military imports.
Japanese companies seem to be
establishing themselves on the
ground floor with a view to taking
over the petroleum distribution
business in China.

The imports of Japanese petroleum products have been
handled principally by Idemitsu, Marruzen, Mitsubishi and Sekiyu Rengo
Kaisha.These companies seem to be laying foundations for an even
larger shere of the Yangtzse trade than enjoyed in the past.
The Sekiyu Rengo Kaisha of Japan has developed an
installetion at Gough Island lying between the Kwang Wha and Texaco
plants for the declared purpose of meeting Army requirements. It is
reported that Icemitsu are drawing supplies from this installation for
commercial purposes. So far one 60' and two 20' tanks as v.cll as
godowns have been constructed. Four 40' tanks are under construction.
The Sekiyu Rengo are not selling commercially but Idemitsu, acting on
their behalf as well as drawing supplies from Japan, Korea end Dairen,
have established selling organizations in Shanghai, Hangchow, Soochow,
Chinkiang, Hankow, Nanking and Wuhu.
The Marruzen Oil Co. of Japan have not established a
selling organization but have developed an installation on Gough
Island adjacent to the A.P.Co. gasoline depot with a water frontage of
some 3,000 feet. At present three 60', three 40' and one 20' tanks
are under construction. It is reported that this installation is to
handle supplies for the Japanese Navy. Whether or not commercial
supplies also pass through this depot remains to be seen.
At Pukow immediately across frcm Nanking and below
our Pukow Installation the Marruzen Company are constructing an
installation, also reported to be for Japanese Navy supplies. Hankow
reports the Sekiyu Rengo Kaisha are making arrangements to develop
an installation near their city to meet military requirements.
The Mitsubishi Sekiyu Kaisha of Japan has not
developed terminal facilities in the Yangtsze Territory but it was
recently reported from Japan that special arrangements had been made
to permit this ccmpany to export refined products tc North China
outside of exports governed by the Department of Commerce and Industry.
The movement of Japanese petroleum products in the
Yangtsze Territory for commercial purposes is not restricted although
shippers are required to secure permits to cover ail transfers. On
the other hand, we cannot as a company move our products out of
Shanghai to any of the Japanese occupied territories except on
applications made by Japanese agents or firms acting as "go-betweens".
This immediately places us at a disadvantage as we must pay for the
services of middle-men. Also the shippers of our products are
breaking all regulations insomuch as the cargo shipped is not owned

In other words, every possible hindrance seems to be placed on our
trade.

The imports of Japanese petroleum products for
commercial purposes have been made from Dairen, Korea, Japan and
California. These imports have been handled largely by Jobbers end
Mitsubishi. The Mitsubishi interests are developing an installation
adjacent to our Tsingtao property oovering en area of approximately
6 acres. Two 3,ooo tons tanks and one filling tank are being erected.
This development is reported to be for Naval use but supplies have
recently been sold through th.is depot for commercial purposes.
Japanese imports have rapidly gained ground in
Shantung through a control of bus lines, railways, mills end all
import and export facilities. Also Naval permits are necessary before
landing any petroleum cargo in Tsingtao. Our interior organization
has been disrupted due to unsettled conditions and restrictions on
shipments that make it impossible to ship requid supplies. We are
permitted to ship in our own name but with preference given Japanese
traders,the outlook is not bright.

The Ta Wha Company owned by Manseiki and Idemitsu
is the only Japanese concern operating as a true oil company with e
developed installation at Hsinho and established branches on principal
markets. Other importers of Japanese products from Dairen, Korea and
Japan have been numerous but have not developed organizations.
It was reported last August that Po Hei, a Sino-
Jepanese concern, had been organized to develop a Refinery in Tientsin
but so far no progress has been made.
The merketine of our products has been serbusly
curtai led by trade restrictions that invariably give Japanese
importers an advantage. At present shipments of our products can be
made from Tientsin if and when shipping space isaveilable without
obtaining permits but unce the cargo reaches destination further

=== Page 11 === (confidence 1.00)
Maxwell M. Hamilton.

February 15, 1940.
movement is strictly limited or prohibited. Also throughout the
greater portion of the territory prices are now fixed by a Control
Board. So far these prices have been fixed at limits that are from 3
to lo F.R.B. dollars below prices we wish to quote. In some instances
agents have been forced to sell at fixed prices resulting in considerable
loss. The lifting of this reguletion would improve the situation
but we would continue to be at a decided disadvantage since petroleum
products refined in Japan are from crude imported at an exchange of
about 23 U.S. cents to the Yen which is at par with F.R.B. currency.
We are forced to sell in F.R.B. notes and remit proceeds at rates of
exchange ranging from 7 to 10 U.S. cents to a local dollar. This
difference in exchange will continue to be an advantage to the Yen
Bloc countries not only in Tientsin but throughout North China until
we have had a currency that can be traded freely et rates level with
the U.S. Dollar/Yen.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
March 23, 1940.
Reference a report from the Tokyo Consu-
late General, February 10, 1940, entitled
"Japanese Monopoly for the Sale of Petroleum
Products".
Effective February 1, 1940, there has
been established in Japan a monopoly for the
distribution and sale of gasoline, kerosene,
diesel oil, and machine oil. Freviously, for-
eign companies have imported refined oil
products, stored them, and sold them to whole-
salers who in turn soid them to retailers.
The gasoline was sold, however, by its brand
name and by dealers selling no other gasoline.
Under the monopoly plan one large company
(Sekiyu Kyohan Kabushiki Kaisha) would pur-
chase the output of Japanese oil companies and
the imports of foreign companies. A "subsid-
iary" of the monopoly would be organized in
each prefecture to replace the former whole-
salers, and these would sell to retailers.
Under the original plan, all gasoline would be
mixed and private brands and trademarks would
disappear from the market. However, foreign
companies thus far have refused to sell to the
monopoly and negotiations are in progress
according to which the foreign companies would
sell their products through the Irefectural
"subsidiaries" but their products would be
stored and marketed separately under their
brand names and by retailers selling no other
gasoline.
There is widespreed opposition among
Japanese

=== Page 16 === (confidence 0.97)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-2-
Japanese retailers to the new system and it
may break down.
The Embassy has not reported upon this
matter and the American oil companies do not
appear to have asked for help. No action seems
to be required at this time.
Incidentally, the despatch under reference
is not well organized and although the situa-
tion may be confused the presentation of the
problem in this despatch seems even more con-
fused.

JAPANESE MONOPOLY FOR THE SALE OF PETROLEUM PRODUCTS
Effeotive as of February 1, l940, there has been
established in Japan a monopoly for the distribution
and sale of gasoline, kerosene, diesel oil, and maohine
oil. The full organization 1s not yet complete and
there are still certain questions outstanding between
the monopoly and the foreign oil companies. It 1s the
purpose of this report, therefore, to desoribe the organ-
ization as at present set up and to state the present
aims of the foreign companies.
Summary
It 1s the aim of the Japanese authorities to estab
lish a system of monopolies for the distribution and sale
of petroleum produots. This system would result in the
disappearance from the Japanese market of brand names.
The American and British oil companies, while unable to
oppose the monopoly in 1ts entirety, may be able to pro-
teot the goodwill which they have established in past
years. There 1s also strong opposition among Japanese
distributors to the new monopolistic system.
Dietribution and Sales Methods Before February 1
Before February 1, 1940, the forelgn companies 1m-
ported refined oil products, but no crude oil, stored
them, and sold them to wholesalers who in turn sold them
to retailers. Japanese oil companies imported orude oil,
refined it, and sold the producte either to wholesalers,
as did the foreign companles, or oocasionally to their
own retail outlets. The area covered by a single
wholesaler

=== Page 20 === (confidence 0.98)
一4-
wholesaler varied. In Tokyo the Amerioan company sold
to three wholesalers, while in the country there might
be only one wholesaler for an entire prefeoture. The
wholesaler frequently owned several filling stations
which he would supply with gasoline in addition to the
independent stations.
Sekiyu Kyohan Kabushiki Kaisha Established
Erfeotive February 1, 1940, the Sekiyu Kyohan
Kabushiki Kaisha was established. The company has
20,000 shares of Y50.00 par value, which are distributed
among the Japanese oil companies on the basis of their
sales prior to February 1, 1940. It was originally
planned that this company should buy the total output
of the Japanese companies from the refineries and the
total imports for civilian consumption of the foreign
companies and act as the sole medium of their distribu-
tion and sale to the prefeotural Sekiyu Hamba1 Kabushik1
Kaisha, whioh will be described later. The sales and
distribution organization of the domestic companies was
taken over in 1ts entirety by the Sekiyu Kyohan Kabushik1
Ka1sha.
The foreign companies, however, were finally able
to persuade the Japanese authorities not to make this
procedure compulsory in their case.
Sek1yu Hamba1 Kabushik1 Ka1sha Organized
The Sekiyu Kyohan Kabushik1 Kaisha 1s a single com-
pany covering all of Japan. In addition there has been
organized 1n each prefecture a Sekiyu Hamba1 Kabushik1
Kaisha to replace the former wholesalers. All the oil
products aoquired by the Sekiyu Kyohan K.K. from the
Japanese

=== Page 21 === (confidence 0.98)
-5-
Japanese oil companies as well as the oil products
1mported for civilian consumption by the foreign oil
companies must be sold through the prefeotural Sekiyu
Hambai K.K. Former direot sales by the foreign oil
companies, except to foreign embassies and legations,
to the Japanese military and naval foroes, and to the
Japan Air Transport Company, are no longer permitted.
The Sekiyu Hamba1 K.K. in each prefecture issues
shares to the former wholesalers in the prefecture in
accordance with their sales prior to February 1, and
1t 1s planned that the dividends from the shares will
compensate the wholesalers for their profits under the
former system. Stations owned by the wholesalers will
continue to be operated by their former owmers, but all
gasoline and other products now have to be purohased
from the prefectural Sekiyu Hamba1 Kabushik1 Kaisha.
The plan thus proposed a direot line of purohase
by the retailer from the prefectural Sekiyu Hambai K,K.
from the Sekiyu Kyohan K.K.
Objections of the Foreign Companies
Under the original plan there would disappear from
the Japanese market all private brande and trademarks.
There has even been talk to the effect that the Sekiyu
Kyohan K.K. would take the gasoline which 1t received
from the various producers and mix it, thus effectively
doing away with any distinctlon. At the same time, fill-
1ng stations equipped with Standard-Vacuum or Shell equip-
ment, while still showing the same signs, would not in
reality be selling the products of these companies.
Both the Standard-Vacuum Oil Company and the Shell,
under the looal name of the Rising Sun Petroleum Company,
have

=== Page 22 === (confidence 0.98)
一6-
have spent a great deal of money over a period of many
years in building up goodwill. This has been done
through widespread advertising of their various trade-
marks and through the sale of products of a consistent-
ly high quality, the latter of whion in particular they
claim has not been true of the products of some of the
Japanese companies.
The foreign companies, consequently, in order to
retain some control over their produots and in an attempt
to maintain the goodwill which they had built up, refused
to sell to the Sekiyu Kyohan K.K. Up to the present,
they have been successful in their refusal, and it is
not at present anticipated that they will be foroed to
sell to the Sekiyu Kyohan, but instead will sell direot-
ly to the prefeotural Sekiyu Hamba1 K.K.
In order to complete their protection, however, it
1s necessary that the stations formerly supplied, direct-
ly or indirectly, by the foreign companies should con-
tinue to receive their products.
Negotiations are at present in progress by which the
foreign companies hope and even expect to secure author-
1zation for a plan whereby they will control the ultimate
destination of their products, even though sold through
the Sekiyu Hamba1. Under this plan they will sell direct-
ly to the Sekiyu Hamba1, which will store the gaeoline
and other produots separately from that received from the
Sekiyu Kyohan, Just as the wholesalers formerly stored it
separately. The Sekiyu Hamba1 will sell this special
gasoline only to the retailere who have heretofore been
selling 1t, the amount which each retailer receives to
be based on the proportion of his previous sales to the
previous

=== Page 23 === (confidence 0.98)
一7-
previous total sales of that brand of gasoline.
If this plan 1s approved, as the foreign oil
companies at present anticipate, it will mean that
their produots will be sold only through stations
whioh show their brand names and that stations showing
their brand names will sell no other gasoline.
Special Condition Relating to Lubricating Oils
The plan put in force by the Japanese authorities
envisages, as was stated before, the sale through the
prefeotural Sekiyu Hambai K.K. not only of gasoline and
kerosene but also of diesel and maohine oils. While the
foreign companies cannot avoid the compulsion to sell
diesel oil through the Sekiyu Hamba1, they claim that
the lubrioating oils which they sell are of much higher
grade than the machine oils embraced in the plan of the
Japanese authorities and that they do not fall within
the specifioations for such maohine oile. For the present,
therefore, they are continuing the direct sale of high-
grade lubricating oils. Whether their contention will
be finally upheld is not yet known.
Determination of Quotas to Retailers
The Fuel Bureau of the Ministry of Commerce and
Industry determines the annual, quarterly, or monthly
quotas whioh are to be allowed to the different companles.
At present quotas are being 1ssued on a quarterly basis.
When the Standard-Vacuum Oil Company, for example,
receives its quarterly quota, it divides the quota into
three equal parts in order to seoure the monthly quota.
This monthly quota, in turn, 1s divided up among the
wholesalers in aocordance with their sales previously.
Starting

=== Page 24 === (confidence 0.98)
一8-
Starting next month, however, when the wholesalers
finally disappear, it is the intention of the company
to caloulate the previous sales of the individual fill-
ing stations and to supply them through the Sekiyu
Hambai K.K. with their proportionate part of the total
quota for the month.
Wholegale and Retail Price Spread
Under the system inaugurated on February 1, gasoline
selling for Yo.64 at the pump 1s sold to the Sekiyu
Hambai K.K. for Yo.60 a gallon. This month the Sekiyu
Hambai K.K. takes Yo.00-l/2, the former wholesaler re-
ceives Y0.0l, and the retailer receives Y0.02-1/2.
Starting with next month, the former wholesalers will
receive nothing, but will depend for his income upon
dividends from his shares in the Sekiyu Hamba1 K.K.
Under the former competitive system, retailers
received from Y0.04 to x0.06 a gallon, depending upon
the 1ndividual case. The cut to Yo.02-1/2 represents
a serious loss to them, and resentment and oppoeition
1e widespread. There is to be a meeting of the leaders
of the various associations of retailers soon, followed
by a mass meeting of protest cn February 12.
Comment Upon the New System
It 1s reported that the present confusion in the
distribution and sele of petroleum products 1s the result
of the Government's anxiety to establish the new syetem
before the reopening of the Diet. An abortive attempt
was made to introduce the new syetem in September of last
year, but failed due to the concerted opposition of whole-
salers and retailers. This time orders were issued
abruptly,

=== Page 25 === (confidence 0.97)
- 9 -
abruptly, just before February 1, and there was no
opportunity to organize an effective opposition.
The Standard-Vacuum O1l Company, due to 1ts other
diffioulties with the Government, is attempting to co-
cperate with the Government in thie partioular, but it
anticipates a w1despread attitude of non-cooperation
amongst the Japanese involved, which may lead to the
breakdown of the new syetem.

The American Embassy,
TOKYO.
Attention: Mr. E. S. Crocker
Dear Sirs:
Although you are of course aware of the stimulation
to Japanese interest in the China Petroleum trade conse-
quent upon the Military activity there, it is possible
that you have not had the opportunity to visualize the
cumulative results of the factors operating in favour of
Japanese interests. Therefore we are taking the liberty
of attaching copy of letter to our New York Office by
our Shanghai Office under date of December 30th, 1939.
We are also attaching copy of a blueprint showing Japa-
nese development on Gough Island which is described in
Shanghai's letter.
Recently our people in Shanghai have had further
oause for concern by reason of Japanese Press reports to
the effect that the verious Japanese oil concerns operat-
ing in China are organizing "Traders' Councils" both in
Contral and North China for the purpose of controlling
their supplies and market prices. Naturally our people
with their background of experience with Japanese methods
of

=== Page 33 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
of penetration are apprehensive that this movement has
far-reaohing aims detrimental to our established inter-
ests. For this reason they wish that the situation be
made known to you with the hope that you may find the
information useful in your defence of American interests.
Yours very truly,
C. E. MEYER
General Manager

December 30th, 1939.
Japanese Oil Development.
Standard-Vacuum Oil Company,
NEW YORK.
Dear Sirs,
Recently we requested our North China Territories to
report on Japanese petroleum activities in relation to our
own trade, and we give hereunder a resume of their findings.
For the sake of convenience and clarity we have divided
the North China Division into the Tientsin Territory,
Tsingtao or Shantung Territory and the Yangtsze Valley,
the latter comprising territories not covered by the first
two.
YANGTSZE VALLEY:
Figures representing trade secured by Japanese

The imports of Japanese petroleum products have been
handled principally by Idemitsu, Maruzen, Mitsubishi and
Sekiyu Rengo Kaisha. These companies seem to be laying
foundations for an even larger share of the Yangtsze trade
than enjoyed in the past.
The Sekiyu Rengo Kaisha of Japan has developed an
installation at Gough Island lying between the Kwang Wha
and Texaco plants for the declared purpose of meeting Army
requirements. It is reported that Idemitsu are drawing
supplies from this installation for commercial purposes.
So far one 60' and two 20' tanks as well as godowns have
been constructed. Four 4o' tanks are under construction.
The Sekiyu Rengo are not selling commercially but Idemitsu,
acting on their behalf as well as drawing supplies from
Japan, Korea and Dairen, have established selling organiza-
tions in Shanghai, Hangchow, Soochow, Chinkiang, Hankow,
Nanking and Wuhu.
The Maruzen Oil Co. of Japan have not established a
selling organization but have developed an installation
on Gough Island adjacent to the A.P.Co. gasoline depot
with a water frontage of some 3,oo0 feet. At present three
60', three 40' and one 20' tanks are under construction.
It is reported that this installation is to handle supplies

=== Page 36 === (confidence 0.99)
-3
for the Japanese Navy. Whether or not commeroial supplies
also pass through this depot remains to be seen.
At Pukow immediately aoross from Nanking and below
our Pukow Installation the Maruzen Company are construoting
an installation, also reported to be for Japanese Navy
supplies. Hankow reports the Sekiyu Rengo Kaisha are
making arrangements to develop an installation near their
city to meet military requirements.
The Mitsubishi Sekiyu Kaisha of Japan has not developed
terminal facilities in the Yangtsze Territory but it was
recently reported from Japan that special arrangements
had been made to permit this company to export refined
products to North China outside of exports governed by
the Department of Commerce and Industry.
The movement of Japanese petroleum products in the
Yangtsze Territory for commercial purposes is not restricted
although shippers are required to secure permits to cover
all transfers. On the other hand, we cannot as a company
move our products out of Shanghai to any of the Japanese
occupied territories except on applications made by Japa-
nese agents or firms acting as "go-betweens". This imme-
diately places us at a disadvantage as we must pay for the
services of middle-men. Also the shippers of our products
are breaking all regulations insomuch as the cargo shipped
is not owned by them so shipments must be curtailed in order
to avoid suspicion. In other words, every possible hindrance
seems to be placed on our trade.
SHANTUNG TERRITORY:

hereunder:

The imports of Japanese petroleum products for com-
mercial purposes have been made from Dairen, Korea, Japan
and California. These imports have been handled largely
by Jobbers and Mitsubishi. The Mitsubishi interests are
developing an installation adjacent to our Tsingtao property
covering an area of approximately 6 acres. Two 3,000 ton
tanks and one filling tank are being erected. This develop-
ment is reported to be for Naval use but supplies have
recently been sold through this depot for commercial purposes.
Japanese imports have rapidly gained ground in Shantung
through a control of bus lines, railways, mills and all
import and export facilities. Also Naval permits are neces-
sary before landing any petroleum cargo in Tsingtao. Our
interior organization has been disrupted due to unsettled
conditions and restrictions on shipments that make it im-
possible to ship required supplies. We are permitted to
ship in our own name but with preference given Japanese
traders, the outlook is not bright.

The Ta Hwa Company owned by Manseiki and Idemitsu is
the only Japanese concern operating as a true oil company
with a developed installation at Hsinho and established
branches on principal markets. Other importers of Japanese
products from Dairen, Korea and Japan have been numerous
but bave not developed organizations.
It was reported last August that Po Hei, a Sino-Japaneso
concern, had been organized to develop a Refinery in Tientsin
but so far no progress has been made.
The marketing of our products has been seriously cur-
tailed by trade restrictions that invariably give Japanese
importers an advantage. At present shipments of our products
oan be made from Tientsin if and when shipping space is

=== Page 39 === (confidence 0.99)
-6-
available without obtaining permits but once the cargo
reaches destination further movement is strictly limited
or prohibited. Also throughout the greater portion of the
torritory prices are now fixed by a Control Board. So far
these prices have been fixed at limits that are from 3 to
10 F.R.B. dollars below prices we wish to quote. In some
instanees agents have been forced to sell at fixed prices
resulting in considerable loss. The lifting of this regu-
lation would improve the situation but we would continue
to be at a decided disadvantage since petroleum products
refined in Japan are from crude imported at an exchange of
about 23 U.S. cents to the Yen which is at par with F.R.B.
currency. We are forced to sell in F.R.B. notes and remit
proceeds at rates of exchange ranging from 7 to lo U.S.
cents to a local dollar. This difference in exohange will
continue to be an advantage to the Yen Bloc countries not
only in Tientsin but throughout North China until we have
had a currency that can be traded freely at rates level
with the U.S. Dollar/Yen.

It can be seen from the above that the volume of our
business and the business of other foreign importers is
dependent on Japanese inability to adequately supply the
North China market. Japanese petroleum exports at present
are limited but nevertheless they have managed to seoure
a very large share of the trade. Just how far they will be
able to go under more normal conditions remains to be seen
but they are certainly laying a foundation that may very
oasily result in their domination of the petroleum markets
in North China.

STANDARD-VACUUM OIL COMPANY

less than that which would be reguired to convert Japen's potential
annual production of 1,480,000 barrels of aviation gasoline base
stock to l00 octane gasoline. To remedy its pparent deficiency
in tetra-ethy1 lead and 1so-octane, Japan imported 965,000 pounds
of tetra-ethy1 lead and 140,000 barrels of iso-octane in 1939 from
the United States. The size of these purcnases, at least of lead,
would indicate an efiort to build up reserve supplies.
6 - Gasoline under aviation grade and the crude petroleum from which
to make it can be purchased by Japan from Saahlin, Netherlands Indies,
British Borneo, Mexico, Ecuador, Peru and Colombia, nd other sources
as well as from the United States. Moreover, Japan possesses a con-
siderable number of fast modern tancers with which to transport these
oils. Stopvage of American exports of petroleum and gasoline (omit-
ting consideration of aviation gasoline), however, would work a hard-
ship on Japan. Brin ing oils fron countries further distant than the
United States (except Netherlands Indies) would eniance the cost of
transport. Also, the Japanese refining industry is ouilt to operate
largely on Celifornia petroleum, and use of crudes from other sources
would probably result in decreased efficiency.
7 - Although much of Japan's milit:r.  ircr it re built to use nigh
grade aviation iuel, under present fi nting conditions in China it is
believed that Japanese aviators have obtained satisfactory results
with gasoline well below l00 octane. If, however, Japan should be-
come involved in war with a first class military power, it would be
handicapped in the air without the best rade of aviation gasoline;
it is probably for this reason that the Japanese have made efforts
to build up reserve supplies of such fuel. It is thouht that with
present stocks and present rate nd type of consunmtion, even if
supplies were cut off from this country, Japan would be able to con-
tinue the war in China for t least another year vithout serious
danger of a short se of petroleu. products.

Referonce Tokyo's despatch no. 4758,
June 7, 1940, entitled "Manufacture of
Synthetio Petroleum". There is enolosed
with the despatoh under reference a brief
momorandum by Mr. Donald W. Smith, Assist-
ant Commercial Attache; in regard to the
progress which has been made in Japan in the
manufaoture of synthetic gasoline. It has
been announoed in the Japanese press that
artificial petroleum was produced by the
synthetio prooesses for the first time in
Japan following the trial operation of the
Miike oil synthesis plant of the Mitsui
Mining Company. According to Lir. Smith, how-
ever, this must not be taken as an indica-
tion that Japan has made any progress towards
becoming independent of foreign sources for
petroleum products. The plant has a produc-
tion capacity of only 1,O0o metric tons a
year, which indicates the plant is merely a
tost or pilot plant.
The Miike plant is not the first plant
to produce synthetic oil in Japan, accord-
ing to Mr. Smith, who has ascertained from
a reliable source that the Nippon Yuka
Company, Limited, has actually been in pro-
duotion for the past five months. The
capacity of the plant of this company is
reported to be about 50,0o0 metric tons a
year and the production is said to be
suitabla

=== Page 58 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-2-
suitable for the manufacture of aviation
gas of no. 85 ootane rating.
The Japanese Government is subsidizing
to a considerable extent the production of
ynthetio oil in Japan. In 1937 the Imperial
VFuel Industry was established with a capital
of yen 100,000,000, half of which was sub-
soribed by the Government. The object of
this conoern is to make investments with a
viow to promoting the artificial oil industry
in Japan. The l940-41 budget contains an
item or Yen 19,018,607 for "Expenditure to
realize 'supply of liquid fuels".
According to a plan drafted by the Gov-
ernment several years ago, a production of
1,028,000 kilo liters of gasoline and
1,139,000 kilo liters of heavy oil was en-
visaged by l943, but last year the plan was
revised to achieve these objectives by the
end of 194l. Since that time plans have
been altered again due to the difficulties
of obtaining plant equipment from foreign
souroes. It is believed that certain equip-
ment is obtainable only in the United tates
at the present time. Even if the plan for
produotion should be attained, Japan would
still be dependent on foreign countries for
approximately 40 percent of her gasoline
requirements in 1943.

With reference to the Enbassy's despatch no. 2337,
RECORDING DE8D
FILE-C.S.
April 1, 1937, on the subject of Japan's efforts to
1/
obtain supplies of petroleum, I have the honor to enclose
a copy of a memorandum on the status of Japan's artificial
petroleum industry prepared by Mr. Donald W. Smith, Assist-
ant Commercial Attaché.
The

=== Page 61 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
The vernacular press on May 3lst announced that
artificial petroleum was produced by the synthetic process
for the first time in Japan following the trial operation
of the Miike oil synthesis plant of the Mitsui Mining
Company. This was aciievec, it was reported, after three
years of effort following the purchase of the synthetic
process said to have been invented by Franz Fischer of
Germany in 1935. Captain Enomoto, Chief of the Artificial
Oil Section of the Fuel Bureau in the Ministry of Comnerce
and Industry, expressed in a statement to the press his joy
over the success so far achieved. He emphasized the im-
portance of an adequate supply of artificial oil to the
German forces in the present operations in Europe and by
implication the future similar importance of Japanese pro-
duction of oil to Japan's military needs. Captin Enonoto
briefly described the synthetic process and asserted that
efforts are being made to develop the process which will
replace the present catalytic agent, cobalt, with iron.
He reported that in addition to the plant ut  iike other
synthetic oil factories are bein built in Takigawa by the
Hokkaido Oil Company, at Chinchow, by the anchuria Fuel
Company, and at other unspecified places. All of these
plants are expected to be com leted and to be in operation
next year.
Captain Euonoto concluded that Japan hopes to
become independent of forei II cou.itries for its supply of oil
through the use or the hyurocenation and synthetic processes.
Mr. Smith

=== Page 62 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
Mr. Smith in his memorandum expresses the opinion
that the success of the plant in Miike should not be re-
garded as an indication that Japan has made any progress
toward becoming independent of foreign sources for petroleum.
It is believed that the itsui synthetic oil factory is merely
a "test or pilot plant". iIe reports that the Japan Oil Hydro-
genation Comnany has been producing artificial oil by the
"Burgess system of coal liquefaction" for the past five
months at a plant located between Tokyo and Yokohana. The
Japanese Government has subsiuized and assisted most of the
firms interested in the production of artificial oil and the
current budget of the i inistry of Commerce and Industry con-
tains an item of some ¥19,000,000 for "expenditures to realize
self-supply of liquid fuels". Mr. Smith emphasizes the de-
pendence of promoters of the artificial oil inaustry on
foreign sources, particularly the United States for their
industrial equipment and states that even if Japan should
succeed in providing for the production of 1,028,000 kilo
litres of gasoline, which is in accordance with Government
plans, Japan would still be required to import 40 per cert of
its gasoline requirements for 1943. In conclusion, r. Smith
believes that little progress has been accomplished toward
the builaing of a large-scule artificial oil industry and that
any action denying to Japan especial equi ment necessary for
that industry woulc seriously hamper the Japanese Government's
plans and make difficult the completion of artificial oil
factories now under construction.
Respectfully yours,
8636
Joseph C. Grew.

=== Page 63 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure no. ' to despatch
from the Embassy at Tokyo.
Tokyo, June 5, 1940.
MEMORANDUM TO THE ALBASSADOR:
The report of the success of the Mitsui Mining Company
in producing the first high-grade synthetic gasoline under
the Fischer system of coal liquefaction at the firm's Miike
plant should not be regarded as an indication that Japan has
made any progress towards becoming independent of foreign
sources for petroleum products. Information concerning the
actual capacity of the Litsui Mining Company's Miike synthetic
oil plant is unobtainable under present conditions, but as
the press reports state that the unit now in production has a
capacity of only 1,0oo metric tons a year, it is assumed by
foreign engineers having a knowledge of ths synthetic oil
manufacturing industry that the unit referred to in the press
reports is merely a test or pilot plant.
While the reports published in the Japanese press during
the past week have inferred that the Mitsui Mining Company
is the first Japanese concern to produce synthetic oil by the
coal liquefaction metiod, it has been ascertained from a
reliable source that the Nippon Yuka Company, Ltd. (Japan
Oil Hydrogenation Company) has actually been in production
for the past five months. This concern has a plant between
Tokyo and Yokohama where it is producing oil bv the Burgess
system of coal liquefaction. The actual monthly output of
synthetic oil by the ippon Yuka Company, Ltd. is not known
but the capacity of its plant is reported to be in the neigh-
borhood of 50,000 metric tons a year. The oil obtained from
the liquefaction of coal by this fiim is said to be suitable
for

=== Page 64 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
for the production of aviation gasoline, although the
octane rating of such gasoline is only about 85, compared
with the American standard of l00.
A number of Japanese concerns are engaged in plans
to produce synthetic oil in accordance with a plan drawn
up by the Government to achieve some degree of self-
sufficency in oil in 1937. At that time an Imperial
Fuel Industry Company was established with a capital of
Yen 100,000,000 - half of which was subscribed by the
Government. The chief object of the concern is to make
investments with a view to promoting the artificial oil
industry of this country and during the past few years
the company has advanced funds to most of the firms inter-
ested in the production of synthetic oil. The total invest-
ments of the Government in the artificial oil industry are
not known, but as the 1940-41 budget of the Iinistry of
Commerce and Industry contains an item of Yen 19,018,607
for "expenditure to realize self-supply of liquid fuels",
it is assumed that the Government has already subsidized
the industry to a consideranle extent.
According to a plan drafted by the Government several
years ago, a production or 1,O:8,000 kilo liters of gasoline
and 1,139,000 kilo liters of heavy oil from artificial oil
was envisaged by 1943, but last year the plan was revised
to achieve these objectives by the end of l94l. Since that
time, however, it has again become necessary to alter the
plans for the  roduction of artificial oil due to the diffi-
culty which has been experienced in obtainin/: the high pressure
plant equipment required by the industry from foreign sources.
One of the leading American steel companies is definitely
known to have supplied the high pressure cylinders required
for coal liquefaction to several artificial oil companies and

=== Page 65 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
in view of present conditions in Europe, it appears that
the United States is the only possible source for this
equipment. It is believed that the Japanese artificial
oil industry is further dependent on the United States
(and Canada) for the materials required for the production
of oil by the Burgess and Fischer systems, as the former
requires a catalyst of nickel and the latter, a catalyst of
chromium.
Even the most optimistic Japanese sources have not
visualized complete independence of foreign sources for
petroleum even if the original plans for the production of
artificial oil are successiully executed. According to the
plan drafted in 1937, which as previously mentioned provided
for the production of 1,028,000 kilo liters of gasoline, it
was assumed that even with an output of this volume, Japan
would still be dependent on forein countries to supply
approximately 40% of her gasoline requirements in 1943.
Furthermore, the total expenditure required an investment of
approximately Yen 7oo million, an amount which would be
difficult to raise under present conditions in view of the
stringency in the money market.
In brief, little has actually been acconplished towards
realizing a large-scale artificial oil industry here and even
greater difficulty is anticipated in securing the necessary
foreign equipment for the completion of the plants now under
construction. Any action which might be taken by the United
Stutes in prohibiting the exportation of the high pressure
cylinders

=== Page 66 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
cylinders required by the artificial oil industry
would, it is believed, seriously hamper the plans for
the development of the local industry.
Donald W. Smith,
Assistant Commercial Attaché.

MEMORANDUM

July 15, 1940.
PETROLEUM DISTRIBUTION IN JAPAN
JUL22 1940
AP
The following outline is given as background in-
ormation for possible use in conneotion with business
hegotiations between Tidewater Associated and Mitshishi.
894.63
A. Organization setup in Japan. Commercial distribu-
tion of petroleum products in Japan (not including distribu-
6363/348
tion for Army and Navy) is divided as follows:
1. Foreign companies.
Standard-Vacuum Oil Company, an American corpora-
tion. Rising Sun Petroleum Company, a Japanese sub-
sidiary of Royal Dutch Shell (largely British capital).
These two companies import their products in the
finished form of gasoline, lubricating oils and kerosene.
These they obtain largely from their refineries in the
Dutoh East Indies, Standard from Sumatra and Rising Sun
from Borneo. The foreign companies formerly did about
50 percent of the total commercial business in Japan, but
SILEI
in recent years this has been reduced to about one third
of the total. They import their own products, store them
in their own installations and until this February distributed
them to retailers for marketing under their own brands.
The only essential difference between the two companies
is
F/N

=== Page 68 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
is that Rising Sun operates as a Japanese company; brings
in its products f.o.b.; and attempts to remit its profits
from Japan which are secured from dividends declared by
the Japanese subsidiary. Standard brings in its products
o.i.f. and includes in this price the profit which it
plans to make on their sale. This makes the problem of
dividend remittance less serious than in the case of
Rising Sun.
Certain of tandard's products, including army and
navy orders, industrial oils, et cetera, are distributed
through an agent, Mitsui Bussan Kaisha. This relationship
has been mutually profitable and has been in effect over
a long period of years. It has given Standard the help of
a company with wide distribution facilities and close gov-
ernment conneotions; the latter having been particularly
valuable during recent years. This working arrangement
may be one reason why Mitsui has not gone directly into the
oil business in Jajan. (Because of Mitsui's widespread
investments, which are often of an unpublished nature, the
possibility exists that it may be indirectly interested in
one of standard's Japanese competitors.)
2. Japanese companies.
The balance of the market in Japan (about two tuirds
of the total) is serviced by a number of Japanese companies,
the largest of which are the Mitsubishi Oil Company and
Nippon

=== Page 69 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
Nippon Oil Company. These companies import crude oil,
principally from Kettleman Hills, California, and process
it in their own refineries in Japan.
Mitsubishi has a working relationship with Tidewater
Associated under which Associated acts as agent in proouring
orude oil in the United States; furnishes technical assist-
ance in Japan; and gives other assistance. As an illustra-
tion, machinery and technical assistance for Mitsubishi's
new oracking plant, a unit in its aviation gasoline production,
were seoured through the good offices of Associated.
B. Fetroleum Distribution under Imperial Fuel Board.
Since the beginning of the "China Incident", oil distribu-
tion in Japan has been subjected to a progressive increase in
governmental controls. In February of this year, there was
established under governmental control and direction a
National Joint ales Company. There were also established
in each prefecture a Prefectural Joint Sales Company. The
announced purpose of tnis setup was to eliminate duplication
of effort and to insure smooth distribution of supplies. The
need for this had arisen from consumption controls which
limited the amount of gasoline each company could market and
the amount which each consumer could purcnase. As a result
of these dual controls some prefectures at a given time would
be entirely out of gasoline supplies while there would be
surplus stocks in other prefeotures.
The

=== Page 70 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
The story of the negotiations in regard to this
control program is of some interest. The original govern-
ment program provided for a system of governmental monopoly
in distribution quite similar to that proposed by the "Man-
choukuo" authorities some years ago, and whioh was rejeoted
by the foreign companies and led to their withdrawal from
the field. Under the proposal the foreign companies would
have sold their products to the joint sales company, s torage
facilities would have been pooled and their products would
have lost their identity. The foreign companies objected
to the two latter provisions.
as finally adopted, the program provides for the
initial sale of the products to the Joint Sale Company;
their resale on a commission basis (one-half percent)
through the prefectural sales organizations to wholesalers.
The Joint ales Company determines the volume of monthly
shipments to each prefecture, collects monies due from the
prefectures each month, and, after subtracting commission,
pays the oil companies for their products which had been
distributed the previous month.
The foreign oil compsnies were allowed to keep their
own storage facilities, and arrangements were made to have
their products marketed under the usual trade names.
C. The

=== Page 71 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
C. The Changing Situation in Japan's Petroleum
Industry.
Several recent developments may materially change the
petroleum situation in Japan.
1. Effects of consumption controls.
Progressive reductions in the amounts of gasoline
permitted for ordinary uses have been placed in effeot as
a measure to conserve foreign exchange. Private automo-
biles are now permitted seven gallons of gasoline per month.
Taxicabs, two or two and one-half gallons per day. One
foreign oil company estimated that its annual volume of
gasoline business for ordinary use had been reduced by
40 percent.
This reduction progran has meant that --
a. There has been a much larger measure of
control and direction by the government over the
oil companies;
b. efineries in Japan are operating on only
a part time basis.
2. Change in the status of the Netherlands ast Indies.
The two foreign oil companies in Japan have controlled
the sources of supply in the Netherlands ast Indies and
have heretofore refused to sell crude oil to Japan on the
grounds that the products refined in Japan from this oil
would compete directly with their own products refined in
the Netherlands Last Indies. The Japanese over a long
period of years have attempted to obtain crude oil from
this

=== Page 72 === (confidence 0.99)
-6-
this souroe and they may now be in a position to bring
sufficient pressure to tear to aocomplish this. If Japan
increases its imports of Netherlands Last Indies petroleum
it appears that the increase will be in the form of crude
oil with a corresponding decrease in the volume of imports
of California crude.
3. Foreign xohange.
The foreign oil companies have had a continuous battle
during the past two years to obtain permission to remit their
funds to the home office Rising Sun has had large yen
balances acoruing from dividends which it has been unable to
remit (up to the time I left Japan in March). tandard has
maintained a policy of not importing products until it has
received a permit to purchase forward exchange contracts
whioh permitted remittance approximately six months after
the arrival of the goods. The company has been under constant
pressure to delay remittance and this pressure has on occas-
sion resulted in delays in shipments.
It is my understanding that Tidewater associated has
required payment in american dollars before shipping crude
oil from California. it was reported tnat in 1938 it had
some blocked funds in Japan (I am not sure whether this was
for patents, technical assistance or dividends). It was
reported, however, that these funds were released at the
beginning

=== Page 73 === (confidence 0.99)
-7-
beginning of 1939.
The exchange policy of the Japanese government in
regard to foreign oil companies has been much more liberal
than in the case of foreign companies handling products
of a less essential nature. This may be due to the fact
that tne government could not see its way clear to eliminat-
ing Japan's dependence on Standard and Royal Dutch Shell.
This situation may change radically if Japan is able to
obtain a large part of its oil needs through direct pres-
sure on the Netherlands East Indies Government.
IBW

Department of State
Mr. Leaper of the Standard Vaouum Company telephoned
me this afternoon to say that his company had been
894.6363
approached by Japanese interests which desired to purchase
about 50,000 drums of a lubricating o1l which would fall
just outside of the definition of that oil the export of
which 1s subject to restrictions under provisions of the
President's Proclamation of July 26. In order to fall
5
under that proclamation, a lubricating oil must have a
viscosity of ninety-five or more and a viscosity index
of eighty-five or more. Mr. Leaper said that it would
be perfectly possible for his company to furnish the
Japanese with an oil which has a viscosity of one hundred
twenty-three and a viscosity index of sixty-three to
seventy-nine which, with a little reworking in Japan
would be perfectly satisfactory for use in aircraft engines.
Mr. Leaper said that he realized that this oil did not fall
within the present licensing restrictions and that his
company therefore intended to quote, since the failure to
do so would s1mnly mean that the business would go to
F
competitors. He said that he wisied, however, to bring
ω
the

=== Page 83 === (confidence 0.97)
-2-
the matter to our attention before taking any action since
his company wished to observe the spirit, as well as the
letter of the law.
In reply I informed Mr. Leaper that there are at the
present time no restrictions on the export of lubricating
oil not covered by the definition set forth in the Presi-
dent's Proolamation. I added, however, that it is my
understanding that the Administrator of Export Control is
considering the advisability of some revision of the
definitions of petroleum products contained in the regula-
tions of July 26 and that I would bring the facts which
Mr. Leaper had just set before me to the Administrator's
attention for possible use in connection with thie study.
A copy of this memorandum has been transmitted to the
Administrator.
6w4
Charles W. Yost

The oil in question was said to have been a low grade of combustible

present year. It was originally Mexican oil. Davis had it in storage
at New Orleans at the Douglas Public Warehouse and Storage Plant,
from which it was loaded on the steamer Laurent Meeus on July 19,1940,
which sailed on the same date.
The consignee was Osawa Shokai of Osaka, Japan, and Osaka,
Japan, was the original destination of the shipment. But after the
ship had sailed, and on August 29th, the consignee directed Davis and
Co. to redirect the boat to Yura,Japam. That was done and the cargo
was actually discharged at Yura on September 8,1940, at 2:15 PM.
Payment to Davis and Co. for the amount involved wes made

confidential and commercial sources, W.R.Davis, the principal in

in 1935, a native American of Ebglish descent. Since about 1923 he

Comparatively little is reported in the way of ney Japanese business or new
Jepanese inquiries and it is believed thrt these buyers have on order about
all the material for wicn they will be able to provice transportotion in the
next several months. A lerge quantity of crude and fuel is still to go forward
from the Pacific Coast and a large amount of aviction crsoline in drums is
still on order in Californio ond in the Gulf. Due to tho extreme secrecy which
continues to surround this situation, it is not possible to get eny accurate
figures. It is believed, hovever, thot et least 4oo, ooo drums of grsoline is
still on schedule to move from the Gulf nd over ?0o,00o drums fron the
Pacific Coast.
3
The principal brake on J panese liftiner is reported to be a bottleneck which
has developed in the transportotion field, perticularly in the case of tankers.
It is estimated that the present Japenese ten ter fleet is capeble of lifting
B/i:
卫
894.636
not over about a maximum of 50.O00 B/D fron the United Stotes, and the Japanese
of course have for the pest several years nugmented this fleet by reguler and
periodic charters of foreign tonnage, principally ritish nd Norwegian vessels.
No British vessels have been availahle to them for some months due to their
requisition by the Eritish Admiralty but they have been able to obtein ample
Norwegian tonnage to setisfy their requirements. It is now very reliably
reported that Norwegian ship owmers absolutely refuse to charter any tanters for
voyeges to Japan on the economic principle that these tanters are today far too
valuable to risk their going into dangerous Oriental water-. There are
expressions of opinion which hold that this refuscl to charter tankers is not
besed entirely on economic principles, particularly when insurance underwriters
evidently do not share the seme alarm beceuse war risk rates have not hed any
substantial increase in the past 30 days. Regaraless of reason, however, the
Japanese are finding it practically impossible to obtsin any outside tankers,
and as a result are unable to consummate a substantial amount of business which they
would like to do.
The reluctence of the Norwegians to charter tan'ters has not as yet extended to

=== Page 119 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 1 to despatoh
No. 5189 of December 101940,
from the Embassy at Tokyo.
Conversation
November 10, 1940.
The American Ambassador, Mr. Grew, with the
Minister for Foreign Affairs, Mr. Matsuoka.
During the course of my conversation today with the
Minister for Foreign Affairs I made inf ormal oral repre-
sentations to the Minister on behalf of the Standard-
Vacuum Oil Company of Japan and the Standard Oil Company
of California who, principally because of pressure by
the Japanese military authorities, must sell their
majority shares of the Bitumuls Company of Japan, Limited,
to the Japanese minority interests.
J. C. G.

=== Page 120 === (confidence 0.99)
The Embassy is informed that the Standard-Vacuum Oil
Company of Japan and the Standard Oil Company of California
jointly own 7,466 shares of the Bitumuls Company of Japan,
Ltd.
The shares have a par value of Yen 25.00 and the
total holdings of the two companies have a par value of
Yen 186,650. Japanese interests own the balance of the
shares - 4,534 or Yen 113,350.
The Embassy is further informed that for various
reasons, but principally because of pressure by the
Japanese military authorities, the Standard-Vacuum Oil
Company must either sell all its shares at once, at
their par value, or the Bitumuls Company will go out of
business. The Standard-Vacuum Oil Company wishes to
obtain foreign exchange for the proceeds of the sale,
although because of the devaluation of the yen its hold-
ings are already less in American currency than when the
investment was made at the time they formed the Bitumuls
Company of Japan, Ltd.
The application for the exchange perait was made of
the Finance Minister on October 2, 1940. Under date of
October 30, 1940, this application was refused by the
Chief of the Exchante Bureuu of the Finance Ministry.
November 10, 1940.

Against the baokground of memoranda and informal
370
representations from the British Embassy on the subject
of preventing accumulation by Japan of new and expanding
reserves of petroleum products, and after conversation
with Mr. Welles and Mr. Hamilton, and with Mr. Welles'
authorization, I called Mr. Walden on the telephone this
morning and talked with him and Mr. Parker.
I referred to earlier conversations on the subject of
the quotas which the Japanese authorities allot to Standard-
Vacuum and Shell (Rising Sun) for importation and distribu-
R
S/JHS
FILED
tion in Japan by those companies; I inquired whether I
had been correctly informed to the offect that answer had
19
had to be given to the Japanese yesterday; and I was giv
reply by Mr. Walden that request had been made by the
Japanese
31°

=== Page 135 === (confidence 0.99)
-2
Japanese for a reply on yesterday but that a reply had
not yet been given. I asked whether it might not be
possible for Standard and Shell to give acceptance to
the Japanese offer and thereafter discover that tankers
for delivery of the oil under reference were scarce or
completely unavailable. Mr. Walden said that Standard
had been holding up its reply to the Japanese while
awaiting information regarding the intentions of Shell.
I said that I understood that Shell was advocating
non-acceptance of the Japanese offer. Mr. Walden said
that Standard takes account of the fact that both
Standard and Shell have long urged upon the Japanese
greater liberality; that they well realize that the
Japanese objective at this moment is to get more oil;
but that they feel that a refusal to accept the offer
would cloud their relations with the Japanese and would
give the Japanese another incentive for new pressures
upon the Netherlands East Indies. Mr. Parker said that
Standard is in a better position in Japan than is Shell,
in as much as Standard is able to get yen out of Japan
whereas Shell is not; were Shell in as favorable position
as Standard, he was sure that Shell would not be advocating
non-cooperation.
Both

=== Page 136 === (confidence 0.96)
-3 -
Both Mr. Walden and Mr. Parker said that they would
be glad to do whatever the Department might indicate that
it desired. I said that I did not want to express a
departmental desire; that I was merely inquiring whether
the oil companies might not get together on a procedure
of giving acceptance to the Japanese offer and then
finding delivery difficult, slow, or completely impossible.
Messrs. Walden and Parker said that they would adopt that
plan and endeavor to get Shell to do likewise. I expressed
appreciation of their responsiveness.

This is the first opportunity I have had since the
894.6363/37

December 26th, to the effect that we hac received an in-
quiry for 100,000 tons of Fuel Oil for the Japanese Navy,
6363
and that I merely mentioned it to you as a matter of in-
terest and to give you an opportunity to make any comments
in case you felt it advisable to do so.
As I told you over the telepone, we had already de-
cided not to make any cuotation for this business, at least

2
On December 30th, we received an incuiry for 10,000
tons of Bunker Fuel Oil for the French Navy at Saigon; we
have also decided not to quote on this inquiry.
Further to our conversation with you on November 29th,
I quote the following extracts from reports recently re-
ceived from China which I think may be of interest to you:
SHANGHAI
"Our reduced September sales of Kerosene is a defi-
nite reflection of increased shipping and sales restrictions
imposed by the Japanese Military. All activities seem to
bear out the fact that no foreign oil was to be imported
until Japanese brands were completely disposed of. Agents
for foreign companies' brands may be ordered to assist in
PS/JHS
moving the Japanese Kerosene as well."
NANKING AREA
"It was indicated to foreign oil dealers that they may
be instructed to deal in Japanese Kerosene in order to dis-
tribute foreign brands. Here as well no foreign brands are
allowed to be imported until Japanese oil is off the market."

=== Page 145 === (confidence 0.99)
Mr. Stanley K. Hornbeck
-2-
1-3-41
HANKOW AREA
"Extremely close watcn and checking by the Gendarmarie
prevented sales of foreign kerosene excent in isolated cases.
Dealers were unwilling to stock anything but a few tins of
foreign kerosene for fear of experiencing difficulty with
the authorities. There can be little hope of increasing our
business in this market until Japcnese stock on hand is liqui-
dated."
TSINGTAU
"Shipping between Tsingtau and all coastal points in-
cluding Haichow is still prohibited by the Jap nese authori-
ties. Railway transportation shows improvement but it is
still almost impossible to move our Kerosene from railway
points to the interior, as the Military Authorities continue
to exercise strict control over this."
TIENTSIN
"Restrictions on the part of the Japanese Militery Au-
thorities on up-country shipments appear to be much severer,
particularly on foreign branas."

DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
March 8, 1941.
Limitation of retroleun Exports
to Japan
Over a period of years the volume of exports from
the united States to Japan of the various categories of
petroleum products has generally increased. This increase
has been substantial but more or less even, with the ex-
894.6363/
ception that the latter half of 1940 shows a very sharp
increase concentrated particularly in gasoline (in con-
tainers). increase in various categories of lubricants
was considerable but not as sharp as in gasoline.
This increase of Japanese takings of petroleum pro-
ducts especially concentrated in gusoline in containers
is unquestionably related to the war and war prerarations.
it is possible that part of the inc ease of exports is
finding its way to Gernany, and it seems certain that the
increase is serving to augment apan's preparedness for
war, if war should occur between  apan and Great britain
or the nited States or both. The question of limiting
these exports is obviously of great importance and of
FILED
great imnediate importance.
心
The possibility of arriving at some formula for limit-
P5
15
ing petroleum products exports to vapan has been carefuthy
/LF
studied

=== Page 153 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
studied in the light of effect upon Japan. It is be-
lieved that any system of limitation which may be arrived
at should be a system applying to countries generally
(not to Japan alone), although in accordance with present
American policy it is to be expected that the Western
Hemisphere, Great Britain, Greece, The Netherlands, and
such countries as might from time to time be particularly
singled out for similar reasons would be excepted from
the restrictions. This study, although based upon the
premise that a system of limitation of petroleum products
exports would apply to countries generally (with the
exceptions mentioned) has nevertheless been directed only
toward effect upon relations with Japan. Its applicabil-
ity or inapplicability to relations vith other countries
would require separate study.
It is recommended that exports for each guarter year
period be limited to one quarter of the 1936 quantities
of united states en orts of petroleum products, category
by calegory in the various categories listed in the official
export returns of the United States Government for 1936,
the quota for each category to be increased, and to be
increased hereafter, by one percent for each quarter year
period to allow for presumed increase of ordinary commer-
cial needs. It is recommended that, because quotas for
aviation

=== Page 154 === (confidence 0.84)
-3-
aviation gasoline could not be availed of under present
regulations, there be established a lunp quota for ~asoline
of all types based unon the sun of the exports in 1936 of
natural rasoline, aviation casoline ane other asoline; and
that, because or chanres in classification of lubricating
oil exports, the lubric:tin oil cuota be a lump uota based
upon the sun or the exports in 193, of all types of lubri-
cating oils.

The way in which the official export statistios of
lubricating oils are kept is not entirely satisfactory for
a study

=== Page 158 === (confidence 0.98)
-
study of this type. It is not possible to state with
assurance from the statistics what types of lubricants
are being exported to Japan. It would appear highly
probable that there are included in the lubricating oils
whioh are ourrently being exported to Japan large quanti-
ties of oils which, by processes of further refinement or
treatment, can be rendered into high-grade products for
the most specialized uses (as for example, in aviation).
It should be particularly noted that the miscellaneous
classification "lubricating oil not elsewhere specified"
has risen to very large proportions. While this classifi-
cation is not broken down to indicate types of oils which
are inoluded, it should be observed that the unit value
has risen conspicuously. The statistics as kept give a
total valuation for the various entries, from which can
be computed average unit valuations. In 1939, the average
valuation for this miscellaneous classification for
lubrioating oils exported to Japan computes to $9.70 per
barrel, and in 1940 to $15.80 per barrel. For comparative
purposes, it should be mentioned that the unit price of
the olassification of cylinder oil (bright stooks) was
$10.60. Technical experts appear to be in agreement in the
opinion that Japan is currently purchasing from the United
States

There are other categories of exports of petroleum
products from the United States to Japan which show in
the official statistics but they are of less importance
and need not be separately considered in weighing the
question of the advisability of imposing the recommended
quota limitations.
It is assumed that other export prohibitions and
limitations which apply to petroleum products (as, for
example, against the export of aviation gasoline and of
metal containers) would continue to apply. The quotas
established would be available only for products the
export of which might be permitted under the current laws
and regulations.

=== Page 160 === (confidence 0.98)
wEll be noted upàn atudy of the figures ta
thas the selestion of 1936 (a year which is roundly
years past, was before the European war crisis, and
before the commencement in 1937 of hostilities in China)
base year for the computation of the export quotas would
not be unfair to Japan as measured by Japan's ordinary takings
over a period of years. The proposed quota system is a rea-
sonable formula for allowing reasonable export to Japan to
meet Japan's normal petroleum products needs. In consider-
ing its effect upon Japan it should be recalled that Japan's
hoavy purohases of lubricants and very heavy purchases of
gasoline have probably been in considerable part motivated by
the expectation that there would be restrictions, and that
the limitation to a more normal quantity would therefore not
be as severe a measure as it might at first appear. There
is nothing to indicate that the high recent rate of purchase
from the United States of lubricants and gasoline is the
moasure of a new scale of continuing current needs to which
Japan has become geared. It is unquestionably related to a
Japanese policy of building up stocks. The dangers inherent
in permitting the amassing by Japan of huge reservoirs of
gasoline, and consequent considerably increased military
potential, are dangers which are obvious.

=== Page 169 === (confidence 0.99)
January 11, 1941.
The problem of regulating, curtailing or prohibiting
exports of petroleum products to countries of the Far
East, especially Japan, is one that involves weighing
of many factors and requires great delicacy in handling.
Any procedure which involves a cutting down of
exports, as regards any commodity, is a procedure which
894.6363/378
has adverse effects in both directions: it makes diffi-
culties for the country which is deprived of the com-
modity and it disturbs the economy of the country which
applies the embargo.
In relation to procedures which affect the petroleum
industry and trade in petroleun, this Government has been
moving very circumspectly. We know that to the Japanese
petroleum is an essential import. We know that to this
country petroleun is an important export. We know that
there are available to Japan many sources of petroleum
other than this country. We know that Japan aspires to
the acquisition of a controlling interest in the economic
PS/BE
and the political life of certain regions in the Far East
in which there lie substantial sources of petroleun. We
know also that there are various other regions in the
world in which the authorities and from which the pro-
ducers are by no means euverse to supplying Japan with
petroleun products.
We

=== Page 170 === (confidence 0.99)
-2 -
We are opposed to Japan's general program of
subjugating neighboring countries by force and estab-
lishing Japanese political control over unlimited
been
areas both on land and at sea. We have/opposing
Japan's efforts in pursuit of those objectives by
various measures on our part short of war. Also, we
are now engaged in a gigantic effort of our own in
pursuit of our defense program, and we are conserving,
by measures which we believe reasonable, this
country's resources.
Thus far, we have interfered with the petroleum
trade only to the extent of prohibiting export of
"aviation gasoline", that conmodity being described
by the formula which is popularly described as
"87 octane". At the time when this restriction was
imposed last summer, the Japanese were in process of
making contracts for delivery to them at an early date
of a huge and altogether extraordinary amount of high-
octane gasoline. The imposition of that restriction at
that moment prevented the consumation of that Japanese
effort. Since then, the Japanese have purchased extra-
ordinary amounts of gasoline approximating but just
unuer the "87 octane" specification. It has been pointed
out to us from many sources that the restriction which
now

=== Page 171 === (confidence 0.98)
-3-
now exists is not preventing Japan from accumulating
huge reserves of aviation gasoline; and it has been
urged upon us from many sources, including large
exporters of petroleum products, that the restriotion
upon exports of gasoline be considerably broadened by
reducing the "87 octane" to a considerably lower
figure.
There was for some time a very important political
reason for lot taking any new action regarding exports
of petroleum products. The Japanese had made a demand
on the Netherlands East Indies for contracts which would
provide for the procuring by Japan from the Netherlands
Last Indies of petroleum products to the amount of
3,150,000 tons per annun for five years. Japan was in
position to bring pressure, including military pressure,
upon the Netherlands Indies authorities. It was con-
sidered that pressures, if brought to bear upon Japan
by processes of interference with exports to Japan from
other countries of petroleun products, would tend to
cause the Japanese to increase their pressure upon the
Netherlands ast Indies. In their negotiations, the
companies which produce in and sell from the lietherlands
East Indies succeeded in producing an agreement whereby,
instead of 3,150,000 tons per annum for five years, they
are

=== Page 172 === (confidence 0.99)
-4 -
are committed to selling to Japan 1,800,000 tons per
annum, much of this on a six-months' basis and some of
it on a twelve-months' basis. It is the belief of
officers of this Department who have most intensively
observed and studied the situation and developments in
the Pacific and in United States-Japanese relations,
both political and commercial, that the interests of the
United States and of American oil companies producing in
and selling from the United States are in a better posi-
tion today than they would be had not the contracts which
were made at Batavia been made. [Note: The United States
Government as such, although there came to it a sub-
stantial amount of information regarding the negotiations
at Batavia, had nothing whatever to do with those nego-
tiations; and at no stage did it express approval or
disapproval, nor did it in any way interpose.]
There were during 1940 extraordinarily large exports
from this country to Japan of petroleum products, espe-
cially of gasoline just under "87 octane". The Government
views this development with regret. During recent months
an increasing proportion of the exports has been going
forward in metal drums. By this process Japan is acquir-
ing large stocks for which she does not have to provide
bulk storage facilities and which she can with maximum
of

=== Page 173 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
of convenience move to points where she is conducting
military operations or at which she is establishing bases
for possible new operations. The American Government
cannot but view this development with regret and with
misgivings.
The Japanese are today continuing in their effort
to conquer China; they are establishing bases in Indochina;
they are engaged in agitation in Thailand and are supply-
ing that country with munitions of war; they are known to
be contemplating new movements of ageression southward--
into and against the Netherlands East Indies and the
general area of which Singapore is a strategic center;
they are in various ways rendering certain types of assist-
suspected
ance to their Axis partners; they are/of supplying and
harboring German raiders in the Pacific; and they are
constantly making threats, of a contingent cheracter,
that they may make war upon the United States.
It is estimated that the Japanese have stored up
reserve supplies of petroleum products sufficient to meet
their needs for a period of from nine months to a year;
also, that the amount of their reserves is being increased
rather than decreased. -- It is easily conceivable, world
conditions bein; what they are, Japanese psychology and
Japanese policy being what they are, and American policy
and psychology being what they are, that a time may come
in

=== Page 174 === (confidence 0.99)
-6 -
in the not distant future when Japan and the United
States will be "at war". In such event, petroleum
products which have been imported by Japan from the
United States will of course be used by the Japanese
for operations of the Japanese navy and the Japanese
air force against the naval and air forces of the
United States.
The American Government has not thus far seen fit
to impose embargoes, except as above recorded, upon
export of petroleum products from this country to Japan.
It has, however, inaicated to the petroleum trade that
it does not look with favor on unlimited or excessive
supplying to Japan of petroleum products. It has hoped
that the petroleun industry would exercise self-restraint
and discreet self-deniel in regard to this trade. It
is believed that American petroleum companies could cut
down very substantially upon their exports of petroleum
products, especially of gasoline, to Japan, without sub-
stantial financial losses to thenselves and without giving
the Japanese tangible grounds on which to base complaints.
(Note: It might be suggested to the present and future
inquirers that two procedures in perticular might helpfully
be adopted:
(1) They might refrain from supplying tankers;
(2) They might discontinue supplyin; of metal containers--
including storage tanks, drums, metal hoops, et cetera.)

Mr. Butler, Minister Counselor of the British Embassy,
379
called on me this afternoon at his request.
Mr. Butler showed me a revised edition of a memorandum
on the Japanese oil situation of which he had brought me an
earlier edition some ten days ago. I had, when he brought
me the earlier edition, pointed out certain inaccuracies in
Y
FILED
statements involving the Department of State and had sug
gested revision thereof. In the new edition the revisions
169
had been made. -- Mr. Butler also showed me a memorandum
PS/BE
without subject heading, which dealt with the subject of
oil policy in relation to Thailand.
Having

=== Page 176 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
Having put these memoranda in my hands, Mr. Butler
proceeded to register, in friendly tones but with ex-
pressions of pained regret, a complaint to the general
effect that his Government had addressed to this Govern-
ment through him and through me, in November last, certain
observations on the subject of oil policy, and had re-
ceived no reply. Mr. Butler said that this situation
reflected upon him and that his Government was pressing
its new Ambassador here to get action where its Chargé
had been unsuccessful. I said to Mr. Butler that it was
a matter of regret to me that any matter regarding which
there were contacts between him and me should be produc-
tive of embarrassment to him, but that if he would review
developments in the matter--which I proceeded to outline--
I thought he would agree with me that the fact that his
Government had received no reply was due to circumstances
over which neither he nor I had any substantial measure of
control. There followed some discussion of the problems,
procedures, possibilities, et cetera, involved; and in the
light of that discussion I suggested to Mr. Butler that
either he or the British Ambassador leave the two memoranda
which Mr. Butler had just shown me with the Secretary or the
Under Secretary of State. After some further discussion,
Mr. Butler left me with the impression that he would suggest
to Lord Halifax that he leave them with the Under Secretary.

V. STEPS TOWARDS SELF-SUFFICIENCY
Announcing a plan to cohieve immediate self-suffioienoy in petroleum,
Japan adopted in l936 a "National Fuel Polioy" and established a Bureau
of Fuel which has steadily pursued its objeotive of self-sufficiency. The
1940-41 budget of the Department of Commerce and Industry inoluded en item
of 19,O18,a07 yen for "expediting self-sufriciency in liquid fuel".
A. Inorease in Domestic Production
The prospeets of inoreasing produotion of natural oil in Japan Proper
are small, for the output has been practioally stationary for many years;
COMPIDENTIAL

=== Page 192 === (confidence 0.98)
fields have not been found and old fields are working out. Some op-
timism prevails with regard to potential fields in Manchuria, but the
enthusiasm which greeted a small find near Fushin (Jehol) in May, 1940,
shows how slight must be the basis for substantial output. Even the
most optimistio plans for increase in domestic production of natural oil
have not foreseen a maximum increase of more than 12% in a three year
period (1937-40); such an inorease is insignificant and in any case im-
probable. Since 1937 Japan's shere or Saghalien production has steadily
deolined (due to Soviet obstruction) and in 1940 was wiped out entirely.
Manchurian oil shale is being treated with increasing success, however,
and the output of 436,000 barrels at Fushun for 1937 may well have been
doubled in 1940. Itis.probable, however, that the industrial self-
sufficiency program of Manchuria will continue to absorb the greater
proportion of the output.
A oostly but more promising method of expanding domestic produotion
consists in teking political control over proven oil producing regions.
Broadly speaking, Jnpan's southward expansion policy, if it is aimed at
aoquiring control in the Netherlands Enst Indies, is a phase of the
genercl policy of expanding "domestic" production and lessening reliance
on "foreign" sources.
B. Synthetic and Substitute Fuels
It is through the development of synthetic fuels nd substitutes for
grsoline that Japon expects the grectust progross towrrds self-sufficienoy.
Considerable advence hcs recently beon made in porfecting procosses for
the reduction of conl and tar to motor fucls; such synthotic fuels ere pro-
duced at a cost well above previling market prices and heavy government
