D. Improvement of Refining Technique
With most minerals the end products are determined by the composition
of the raw material. So long es crude oil was treated by the straight-
run distillation process this was true of the petroleum industry, for the
proportion of gasoline, fuel oil, lubriseting; oil and other products was
a result of a physical separation. In the past ten yeurs, however, new
techniques have been developed: (a) The "cracking" process converts heavier
products of streightrun distillation into lighter produets by chemioal
reaction. (b) Polymerization of gcsses resulting from cracking converts
them into high grade gasoline. (c) Hydrogenation of heavy residues pro-
duces further valuable products. How, in the petroleun industry, the end
products may bo largely determined in advance and secured from almost any
given crude oil. Quantitatively, this improvement shows up thus in U. s.
xperienae; whereas in 19lc refining gave a grsoline yield of 25% from a
certain crude, now cracking yiclds 60-65% end more advenced processes may
yield 80%. Jepan's repid development of refinin; ccpacity in rocent years,
her strenuous efforts to secure maximum value from limited resources, end
her search for synthotic fuels, hsve tiven her a modern industry using the
most advenced techniques and securing high returns fron base materials.
(For detcils on refining capacity and methods, see 409-300).

On May 1, 1938, the Fuel Board of the Japanese Ministry of Industry
and Comnerce put into operetion a card system of rationing for all
oil supplies (except Governmeut, Army, Navy and Diplomatic vehicles).
The daily average quota for trucks, cars and busses in Tokyo was set
at 3 gallons per day. The quotas were designod to effect a saving
of 40% in the consunption of the Tokyo areu and of 60% in other
areas. Fishing boat consumption in the Tokyo Bay area was cut 37%
by this ruling. These savings-were to be inoreased by greeter use
of alcohol mixtures ufter July 1, 1938. In May, 1940, these quotas
were drastioally reduced; priyate cars were assigned 3/lo gallons
per day and ordinery busses 2s gtllons per d:y with other types in
between these quotes. In September, 1940, the Tokyo Motropolit:n

gas; in 1939, 2,148,000 barrels were used.
F. Conclusions
The most optimistic of official sources in Japen do not contemplate
even approximate self-sufriciency in petroleum before l943. Meantime,
Japan is attempting to reduce her inoreasing dependence on the U. S. sup-
plies by seeking out other alternative sources. Reports indicete that
Japan has made efforts, especially since the ebrogation of the U. S.-
Japan Treaty of l9ll, to initiate or increase puroheces in N.E.I., Peru,
Eouedor, Colombia, Mexico, Iren. It is noted also that in recent months

Mr.' Walden's attached letter and its
emslosures are of considerable interest.
The figures set forth in the enolosures
sonfirm Mr. Walden's impression which he
stated te me some weeks back that the years
1935-1936 were years in which Japanese 1mports
of petroleum products were abnormally high and
fin exeess of then current domestic consump-
tien. The excess was due to the policy adopted
about that time by the Japanese Government o
requiring those in the oil business to main-
tain large stocks.
The figures for the years 1937-1940
are also of 1nterest--those for 1937-1939
inelusive confirm our assumption that in
those years Japan was endeavoring to build
up still greater stocks by importing well
in excess of current consumption.

follows:
Permit received to take up August

PS/MCS
Mr. Walden said that he understood this messago
meant that the requirements which his company had laid
down for continuing further normal quota business in
Japan had been met [see my memorandum of conversation

Mr. Walden's

the Japanese authorities that unless his company's
blooked yen balances were paid off, his company would
not be willing to continue further sales.] I assume
that the amount mentioned in the cable Mr. Walden
received from Yokohama covers the total blocked
balances of his company, although I am not sure of
the details of this matter.
Mr. Walden also said that he assumed that the
Shell Company had received similar assurances.
Mr. Walden said that he wanted to know whether
when this remittance was received in New York tomorrow,
be
his company would/authorized to receive it. He said
that he further wished to know the proper procedure
with respect to applicet1on for an "unfreezing" license.
I told Mr. Walden that this seemed to me entirely
a matter of Treasury fiscal policy, that I knew that
they were giving serious consideration to the question
of working out some uniform policy that would protect all
American creditors alike, and that I thought he should
talk directly about the matter to the Foreign Funds
Control officials in the Treasury Department. Mr. Walden
asked me if I would mind calling those officials and ask-
ing them to telephone to him about the matter. I agreed
to do this.
I thereupon

=== Page 216 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
I thereupon consulted Mr. Fisher of A-A, Mr. Sohmidt,
Mr. Jones and Mr. Mackay of FE, who were all in agreement
that if the Japanese interests involved were prepared to
furnish free dollars to make the suggested payment, this
Department would have no objections.
Such a payment would be within the scope of the
assurances which Mr. Dean Acheson has given the Japanese
Embassy with respect to exports of petroleum from the
United States to Japan. On the other hand, if the Japa-
nese interests propose merely to instruct the Yokohama
Specie Bank to pay out of an already blocked account to
Mr. Walden, different issues are raised.
In the first instance, payment from a blocked account
would raise directly the question of uniform treatment of
American creditors, a matter over whioh the Treasury has
primary jurisdiction and to which it is currently giving
consideration.
Mr. Fisher felt that in addition, since the liquidation
of Standard-Vacuum's blocked yen balances had been made a
condition precedent to further exports by Standard-Vacuum
from the N.E.I. to Japan, such an application would be
within the scopeof Mr. Dean Acheson's instructions that
for the time being blocked balances could not be used as
payment for exports of petroleum products.
It

=== Page 217 === (confidence 0.95)
-4
It was agreed that in Mr. Acheson's absence it would
be wise for the Department to take no position on this
latter point. Accordingly, I called Mr. A. U. Fox of

informed him of Mr. Walden's call. Mr. Fox said that
he would be glad to cell Mr. Walden and discuss the
matter with him directly. Mr. Fox said that he was in
agreement with our view that if the proposal is to
Foris sgenis.
ntqu

Baron van Boetzelaer called upon me at his request and
said that the Legation had received a telegram raising some
questions about our policy of granting export licenees for
petroleum shipments to Jepan while refusing to grant the neces-
sary payment licenses for the seme exports under the freezing
regulations. He said that his Government had expressed a
reluctance to adopt a similar practice for fear that it
P
might lead to unneceseary misunderstanding with the Japaneee.
S
/MC
He explained that the Japanese might think that they were
entitled to export shipments for which export licenses had
S
been granted and would then feel unnecessarily irritated
if they found that due to payment restrictions thie was
not

=== Page 219 === (confidence 0.99)
-2
not the case.
I told Baron van Boetzelaer that as a matter of fact
according to my understanding we had 1ssued only three export
licenses for permitted petroleum shipments and that
thereafter in the course of conversations with representatives
of the Japanese Embassy on the 1ssue of payment it had been
more or less mutually agreed thet as a matter of practice
we would no longer issue export licenses until the manner
of payment for the particular shipments involved had been
agreed to. I eaid that it eeemed to me that if his Govern-
ment preferred not to issue export licenses until all
matters relating to the shipments under consideration had
been clarified, there would be no divergence of fundamental
policy from our practice. [In this connection we have been
notified by the British Embassy that the Britieh intend to
make their export licenses the final act of control over
all shipments to Japan. Messre. Stopford and Dent have said
that they understand this to mean that before an export
license is granted all neceesery payment requiremente and
shipping requirements will have been met.]
Baron van Boetzelaer went on to say that he felt the
important point was that there be no shipments in fact
without full understanding of the policy involved between
his Government and the American Government. He said that the
Netherlands

=== Page 220 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
Netherlands Government had thus far refused to permit
shipments on three tankers which have called at Indies
ports and that one of these tenkers sought diesel oil at
Tarakan which was of a quality and quantity that placed it
within any Netherlands quota that might be established as the
equivalent of even the reduced quota for diesel oil which
the United States has under consideration. This refusal
had been based on the fact that we had not permitted exports
of similar oil because of our financial requirements even
though export licenses therefor had already been 1ssued by
us. [In connection with the Netherlands East Indies attitude
on payment matters see note at end of this memorandum.]
Baron van Boetzelaer then said that he had further been
informed that the Netherlands Indies Government, which as
we had previously been informed considers that our quotas for
crude oil, diesel oil and fuel oll are too high, is willing
to reduce its comparable over-all quota for permitted exports

rest of the year 194l this f1gure would mean 60,000 tons of

fuel o11, 30,000 tone of crude, and 40,000 tons of diesel
o11.
Baron van Boetzelaer

=== Page 221 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
Baron van Boetzelaer then said entirely on his own
initiative that he believed that his Government would be
prepared to cut off all exports of petroleum if the United
States were to adopt a similar policy although he thought
that such action would raise political questions for dis-
cussion between his Government and the American Government
before the action was finally decided upon.
I told Baron van Boetzelaer that I would convey the
1nformation which he had supplied to Mr. Acheson and others
in the Department interested in the matter. He said that
he would like to call upon Mr. Acheson to discuse the question
because his Government was anxious that a definite policy
be arrived at as soon as possible. He said further, however,
that he was trying to collect a good deal of other related

what imports into the Indies from Jepen the Indies Government

to receive shortly from his Government information as to
whether

=== Page 222 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
whether any offioial announcement of export policy had been

weeks. [We have been informed by Mr. Grew, see Tokyo's
telegram number 1310, of August 26, that the Dutch Indies
authorities have recently expressed to the Japanese Consul
General in strong terms their adherence to a policy of
hereafter forbidding the exports of ell oil to Japan unless
Japan publicly declares that she does not intend to attack
the Indies and unlees Japan withdrars its troops from
southern Indochina. According to Mr. Grew's information
this statement was confirmed to General Pabet, the Netherlands

Baron van Boetzelaer hopes to obtain more definite information

exports to Japan of other important commodities such as
rubber and tin.
[Note: We have heard from a number of sources that the
Indies Government wieshes to cause the Japanese to use up
their present blocked guilder balances in the Indies in
order to be in a better position to require the Japanese to
supply such articles as the Netherlands Indiee find it
necessary to import from Japan. In this connection it
seems relevant to point out that, according to information
received

=== Page 223 === (confidence 0.97)
-6
received from the British Embassy, the Government of India,
which also desires certain essential imports from Japan,
has adopted a different policy in order to accomplish the
same result. The Indian Government will not permit the use
of blocked Japanese funds in India to pay for exports to
Japan but requires fresh imports from Japan as a source
of purchasing power for exports to Japan. It would seem
that this policy is more likely to be effective in compelling
the Japanese to supply needed imports than is the current
Dutch policy and is at the same time less likely to appear
to be at variance with our own freezing policy vis-à-vis
exports to Japan.]

MEMORANDUM FOR THE FILES
September 30, 1941
Mr. Nishiyama called at 2:30 p.m. this afternoon and was
received by Messrs. Kehl and Fox. Mr. Nishiyama inquired if any
decision had been reached in respeot to his last proposal for
making payment for the pending oil shipments to Japan and indi-
cated that the continued delay was quite inconvenient sinoe it
necessitated holding Japanese tankers in West coast ports. It
was indicated that no decision had been reached in respect to the
most recently proposed methods of payment but that the problem
was receiving the consideration of the policy committee.
Mr. Nishiyama pressed for a date on or before which the
Treasury would render a decision and evidenced considerable
anxiety in respect to the Japanese tankers remaining in West coast
ports. He was informed that it was impossible to specify a dead-
line but that a decision would be reached as soon as possible.
Mr. Nishiyama then drew attention to an application of the
Yokohame Specie Bank in respeot to sinking fund operations of the
Taiwan Electric Power Company which he seemed to have the im-
pression had been denied, although it is possible that Mr. Nishi-
yama has construed a request for additional information as a denial.
Mr. Nishiyama was told that this particular case would be investi-
gated but that consideration would be expedited if he could advise
us the Federal Reserve number assigned to it.

I.
Refining Capacity
A. Total Capacity
In 1940 Japan (including kanchuria) imported approximately 21,009,000
barrels of crude oil. It is estimated that the full (lo0%) charging capacity of
al refineries is 32,000,000 barrels/year of crude oils. Actually a few plants
are operating at as much as 70% capacity but the average is 50%. In 1939 Japan's
refineries produced 66% (2,510,000 barrels) of her wartime gasoline needs and in
1941 41% of her total petroleum requirements. Japan's lubricant refining capa-
city is estimated at 2,200,000 barrels a year, or 2/3 of her total supplies
(3,300,000 barrels, including 1,100,000 barrels of imports) in 1940.
B. Aviation Gasoline
The manufacture of aviation gasoline involves processes and materials not
required in the refining of crude oils for their ordinary end products. The
manufacture of low-count octane (&6-90) gasoline, suitable for use in older-
types of airplane motors, requires a selected base stcck gasoline to which is
added tetraethyl lead. Hig: count octare (94-lc)) gascline, necessary for use in
modern airplane motors, requires a seleated base stock gaso ine to which is added
a blending agent such as iso- tane, neo-heyane, cr iso-pentane. Production or
availability of tetraethy] lrad and ble.  ing ar cnts is one of the bottle-necks
in Japanese procurement of high octane count aviation gasolines.
Base Stock: It is estimated that Ja an's maximum current capacity for the
production of aviation gasoline base stoc!: is 1,l80, c00 barrels/year-assuming
plants operating at s0% capacity; actua cutput pr bably reaches little more than
half this figure. Japan has rclied largely on aports of U.S. selected crudes
from which to refine aviation base stock; an ins ni icant amount of special
crude was imported from N.. .f. and British Bornec. The t ansformation of exist-
ing ordinary gasoline refini.? equipment for se in producing aviation gaso-
line base stock would entail a sacrifice in output or ordinary gasoline, for
cracking units applied to production of base stock are only 50% efficient in
terms of quantitative cutput.
Tetra-ethyl lead: To convert her recent inports of base stock gasoline
into high-octane gascline, Japan needs 660,o00 lbs. of tetraethyl lead. Japan
has only one small tetraethyl lead plant in operation; its output is known
but is regarded as insignificant. In 1939 Japan imported 965,0001bs. of 1ead,
but it is probable that she has, since 1938, vastly increased her stock reserve
of lead, without which she was almost wholly dependent on imports from the
United States.
Iso-octane: In addition to the tetraothyl ltad mentioned above, Japan's
current annual output of base-stock requires, for conversion to high octane
gasoline, 518,o00 barrels of iso-octane, a blending agent essential to pro-
duction of high octane gasoline. The usual process of manufacture is by hydro-
genation, although U.S. producers employ a more efficient process (alkylation,
reation of iso-butane and iso-butylene). At present Japan can produce 243,000
barrels/year of iso-octane, or 47% of her actual requirements if all her avail-
able base stock were converted into o7-lo0 octane gasoline.
In 1939 Japan imported from the U.S. 140,000 barrels of iso-octane, an
amount sufficient to meet only 30% of her needs. It is known that Japan is using
87 octane aviation gasoline for military purposes (lcO-octane seems to be stan-
dard only for U.S. and G.B. military aviation; Germany and France have used 87
octane gasoline for military purposes.) In 1939 Japan imported 562,000 harrels
of aviation gasoline from the U.S. in addition to these "boosters".
Resources: Almost the entire consumption of aviation gasoline in Japan
is by naval andmilitary plancs. In 1937 the estimated consumption was 469,000
barrels; in 1938, 742,000 barrcls; in 1939, 791,000 barrels; in 1940, 833,000
barrels. It is believed, in the light of these figures, that Japan has built
-2-
CONFIDI.NTIAL

=== Page 244 === (confidence 0.98)
TIAL
A substantial stock reserve of aviation gaoline or its ingredients - tetraeth
lead, iso-octane, and aviation gasoline base-stock. These items come almost
entirely from the U.S.
Experiments since 1937 have convinced some Japanese experts that some of
their own oil fields can produce napthene base crudes, which show an octane count
of 75-79 on refining and are susceptible of being processed to 94-octane by tho
addition of O.l% tetraethyl lead. Advanced types of aviation motors require a
high octane fuel and Japanese reliance on lead treatment of base stock is apt to
decline in favor of advanced techniques for production of lo0-octane gasoline,
sueh as polymerization and hydrogenation of certain volatile cuts in refining
crude oil. These processes are still U.S. secrets (e.g. Houdray process). Avail-
able figures indicate that Japan is seeking self-sufficiency by rclying wore on
technical advances and on stocks of ingredients than on stocking aviation gasoline;
this is practical, insofar as high octane gasoline can be stored longer than six
months only in "cromak" drums or tanks and evcn these are no assurance against
deterioration after 18 months.
The varied proportions in which anti-knock boosters can be mixed with basc

Since consumption is cstimated at less than 1,O00,000 barrels in 1940, it is clear
that Japan's n't addition to stock rescrv's in r cent yt ars must have been sub-
stantial. Japan's ccmplct: inability to produs avi?tion lubricants is a more
serious bottleneck for it is believed thet Japan has no facilities at all for
domestic production.
II. Synthetic Pctrolcum
A. Athods of inufacture.
Production of syathctic petrolcur ref rs to thos. processes by which non-
petroleum bases arc recucea to petrolcum products. Th. following processes arc
in use in Japan and r s. arch is constantly being carried on under official en-
couragement and subsicy in the hope of developing nw and improved processes.
(1) Low Tempurature Carbonization
Coal is heat d to 500C and then dry distilled. This process yields 3
gals. of gasolinc per ton of coal, plus 180 pounds of other fucl oils. About 70%
of the resultant is "coalite" - a low-grade solid with little markct in Japan;
thc high yield of uscless coalite is one important rcason for the uneconomic
character of this process and of thc continuing nced for government subsidies.
Thc low-temperature process may be used to hydrogenate tar, as is done at a plant
operating in manchuria (Fushin). Japancso havc developed this process by modifying
the standard Bcrgius process and cquipment. In April, 1936, it was rcported to
havc been used satisfactorily for thrce years.
(2) Hydrogenation
This process consists fumdamentally of the dircct liquefaction of coal by
application of heat, pressure, and catalysts. Production costs as late as 1937
were estimated at 70-80 sen/gal. of gasoline so produced, whercas the market
price was 50 scn. This high cost of production xplains the need for subetan-
tial government subsidies. Japan is likely to push this source of synthetic
fuel, for she has ample supplies of coal suitablc for hydrogenation, th: open-
cut mincs at Fushun, h.anchuria, are the largest and cheapest to operate in
the world and large supplies are availablc in Korca and in narthern Kyushu.
At least thrce standard procusses of hydrogenation are used in Japan:
(a) I. G. (Bergius), invented in Germany, 1913. Coal is heated to
400º c under prossure of 200 Atmospheres; heat is advanced to 500° C; cata-
lyst is unknown - possibly iron axide. One ton of liquid fuel is derived from
4-5 tons of coal. Equipment is expensive to install and operate, but gasoline
-3 -
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 245 === (confidence 0.98)
FIDENTIAL
product is good (70 octane); lubricating and Diesel oil products are low grade.
(b) Fischer-hoph method, using atmospheric pressure, less expensive
equipment and operation than Bergius. Product is heavier, more duitable for
Diesel than for gasoline engines (40 octane); quality of lubricating oil is good.
(c) The "Naval Process" has been developed since 1921 by the Japanese
Navy in cooneration with the S.M.R. Co. in experiments at the Japenese Naval
Fuil Depot (Tokuyama). At the beginning, attempts were made to improve the
Fischer process, using Japanese coal. In 1928 Fushun coal was found more satis-
factory; 50 new kinds of catalysts were tried. Then powdered coal mixed
with low temperature tar to form a paste was used as a base and treated with
hydrogen. By 1932 a theoretical 67% of the weight of the coal was returned in
gasoline (67-69 octane) end heavy oil. In February, 1939, experiments were
shifted to a plant near the Fushun coal. field in Ianchuria. In July, 1939, it
was announced that the process ws ptrfected and ready for large scele commer-
cial use.
Actual opcration yiclds crude oil cqunl in wcight to 50% of the coal used;
bituminous coal is most fficiently us d; high tcmperature and pressure are

features of the Fischer prorcss; ? fentur. of quirnent installcd in 1938 at
Supingkai (tanchuria) is cnat a minor modification (climination of coal distil-

and refining. This reans th the unit vill uoe low grade crudes when aveilable,
but can rcadily revort to coal tir disti'lation - hydrogenat,on if crudes are

(3) Synthutieation nd Substitution
On Kay 31, 190, the rtificial Cil Section of the Fucl Burcau (Ministry
of Commerce) nnounced that the  iike oil synthetic plant (lMitsui) had succecded
in producing artificial petroleum by a true :yithosis (synthctization of clcments)
This came after three years of effort following the purchase of the Fischer pro-
cess invented in Gerrany, 1935. Further improvement as expected after present
catalytic ag nt (cobri:) was ruolaccd by iron. In cddition to this "test" or
"pilot" plant at Miihe, other plrnts were boing built at Tokegawa (Hokkaido Oil
co.), Chinchow (Ianchuria Fuel Co.), and other unspccified places, all to begin
commercial opcrations in 1s4l. Th existing plant at Iiike has an alleged
capacity output of 35,o00 barrels/ycar. This announcer ent is the first sign that
the production of petrolcum by true synthetization (carbor. plus hydrogen) has
actually gone beyond tnc laboratory stage. In July, 1940 actual output for
commercial us€ as nil.
The synthetic petrolcum industry of Japan has relied heavily on the im-
portation of plant equipment from Germany and the United States. Aftcr 1939
Japanese plants were largely dependent on imports of plant cquipment from the
United States, especially high pressure cylinders and catalysts (nickel and
chromium).
Since 1932 attempts havc been made to utilime petroleum substitutes. Most
of the serious effort has becn directed to the use of alcohol, both as a pri-
mary fuel and in mixtures (with gasoline and benzol) for usc in airplane motors,
autos, and submarines. The only producers of alcohol in Japan are sugar manu-
facturers (chiefly in Formosa), who use molasses as a base material. The total
annual productive capacity was estimated at 200,000 barrels in 1933, but actual
production was only 98,000 barrcls. In 1939 actual output, available for fuel,
was ostimated at 200,000 barrels. It is known that small plants have been
established in Manchuria (Harbin); stores are kept at Army and Navy eresenals.
Atmmpts are being made to derive alcohol from acetylene, rice, potatoes and
sweet potatoes, but Formosa sugar molaases reains the chief source. Some
success has been achieved also in the use of alcohol as a solvent to extract
gasoline, fuel oils, and lubricants from soya bean oil. Both Army and Navy
have experimented with a mized fuel, containing ordin rily 15% alcohol (pre-
ferably methyl) and 85% gasoline; fuel for army tanks and trucks contains as
much as 30% alcohol.
In 1937, the Japanese Government made the production, sale and importation
of alcohol (ethyl) a Government monoply; by the sane law, gasoline sold after
April 1, 1938 must contain alcohol in a proportion beginning at 5% and increasing
-4-

=== Page 246 === (confidence 0.98)
NTIAL
20% in 1944.
(4) Shale Oil Distillation
The distillation of oil from oil-bearing shale is, strictly speaking, an
extraction rather than a synthetic process. The process has been adapted to
the exploitation of vast oil-bearing shale deposits at Fushun, Manehuria, where
distallation plants are operated by the S.M.R. Co. In June, 1937, the full
capacity output for these plants was estimated at 560,000 B/year of fuel oil
and 84,000 B/year of gasoline; actual production in 1937 was 436,000 barrels
of oil products. A new plant, scheduled for completion in 1938, was designed
to increase total capacity output to 2,100,000 B/year. Actual output in 1939
probably did not exceed 700,000 barrels of oil products.
The oil shale process is relatively costly, but the shale resources are
vast. Fushun shale yields 4-14% in oil :nd recerves arc estinated at 320
million tons of shale (70,O00,o0o barrels of oil). Low teaperature distillation
is used, the oil supplying the fuel. For 10 years the output has been purchased
at cost by the Japanese Navy under a guarantee arrangenert.
Excessive sedinent found in heavy fuel oil derived fror. shale has oaused
trouble in the following ways:
(a) Heavy scdiment found in storage tanks.
(b) Sediment is still excessive when product is rixcd with
heavy petrolcum. oil.
(c) Nozzles, particularly in Diesel engines, bccome clogged.
Special treati cnt is now being used to rer edy this
defcct.
It must be recognizcd that considerable progress has becr. mnde in Japan
since 1936 in thc ficld of synthctic petroloum manufacture and supplies of coal
suitable for direct liquefaction re lerge. Moreover, governient subsidies have
been generous (1940-41 budget provides 19,000, 000 yen for "cxpenditurcs to
realize solf-supply of liquid fuls") nd thc possibility of an e:bargo on
overseas supplius of oil his giv n a preat inpetus to cfforts to cxpend facilities.

(4) Shale Oil Distillation
The distillation of oil from oil-bearing shale is, strictly speaking, an
artraction rather than a synthetic process. The process has been adapted to
the exploitation of vast oil-bearing shale deposits at Fushun, Manehuria, where
dist llation plants are operated by the S.M.R. Co. In June, 1937, the full
capacity output for these plants was cstimated at 560,000 B/year of fuel oil
and 84,000 B/year of gasoline; actual production in 1937 was 436,000 barrels
of oil products. A rew plant, scheduled for completion in l938, was designed
to increase total capacity output to 2,100,000 B/year. Actual output in 1939
probably did not excecd 700,000 barrels of oil products.
The oil shale process is relatively costly, but the shale resources are
vast. Fushun shale yields 4-14& in oil nd reserves arc estinated at 320
million tons of shale (70,000,000 barrels of oil). Low temperature distillation
is used, the oil supplying the fuel. For 10 years the output has been purchased
et cost by the Japanese Navy under a guarantee arrangenent.
Excessive sedinent found in heavy fuel oil derived fror shale has oaused
trouble in the following ways:
(a) Heavy scdiment found in storage tanks.
(b) Sediment is still excessive when product is nixed with
heavy petrolcum oil.
(c) Nozzles, particularly in Diesel engines, become clogged.
Special treatcnt is now being used to rer edy this
defect.
It must be recognizcd that considerable progress has becr. nade in Japan
since 1936 in thc ficld of synthctic petrolcum manufacture and supplies of coal
suitable for direct liquefaction ere lerge. Moreover, governient subsidies have
been generous (1940-41 budget providcs 19,000,000 yen for "cxpenditurcs to
realize self-supply of liquid fucls") and the possibility of an e:bargo on
overseas supplies of oil his given a preat inpetus to cfforts to cxpend facilities.

Conclusions and basic estimateare in many cases
founded on incomplete data, but all available information
EE
is given.
Japan is stated to have 409 manufactures and 300
refineries in its petroleum industry.
The total refining capacity is estimated at 32,000,000
barrels/year of crude oils. In 1939 Japan's refineries
produced 66 per cent of wartime gasoline needs (needed
3,765,000 barrels - produced 2,510,000). In 1941 Japan's
refineries produced 4l per cent of total petroleum requirements
Japanese refineries produced 2/3 of its total supplies of
lubricants in 1940 (2,200,000 bare1s produced, 1,100,000
894.6363
barrels imported).
Production or availability of tetraethyl lead and
blending agents is a "bottleneck" in Japanese procurement
of aviation gasoline.
Japan's maximum productive capacity of aviation gasoline
base stock (to which tetraethyl lead and other blending agents
are added) is estimated at 1,4 0,000 barrels/year at 90
per cent capacity operation -- actual output probably only
1/2 this amount.
E
Japan probably has vastly increased its stock reserv
FILED
1
of tetraethy1 lead since 1938. 965,000 pounds were im-
0
1942
ported in 1939. Japan would need 660,000 1bs. of lead to
treat

=== Page 275 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
treat recent imports of base stock gasoline. Production of
the one known Japanese tetraethyl lead plant is considered
insignificant.
In addition to above-mentioned lead Japan would
need 518,000 barrels of 1so-octane to convert to av1ation
gasoline its current output of base stock gasoline. Japan
can produce 243,000 barrels/year of 1so-octane -- 47% of
amount needed to convert all 1ts base stock.
It 1s believed that Japan has acoumulated a substantial
stock reserve of aviation gasoline or its ingredients.
Japan appears to be seeking self-sufficiency in aviation
gasoline by relying more on technical advances and on
stocks of ingredients than on stored aviation gasoline.
(In 1939 Japan's imported materials and domestic produo-
tion made available about 2,500,000 barrels of aviation
gasoline - In 1940 consumpt1on wasonly 1,000,000 barrels)
Japan 1s believed to have no facilities for domestic
production of aviation lubricants.
Japan has lavishly subsidized synthetio petroleum
production and has encouraged and/or required development
and use of other fuels such as alcohol and shale oil.
Synthetic o1l production of 36 plants in Japan proper
and 1ts possessions (Sakhalin, Korea and Manchuria) 1s
estimated at 8,613,000 barrels per year.

JAPANESE OIL POSITION
(Summary of certain significant estimates
and observations set forth in materlal compiled
by Mr. Thornburg under the above heading.)
1. It is estimated that Japan's
annual consumption of petroleum products
on the basis of all-out war with the
United States, Great Britain, China and
894.6363/404
the Netherlands East Indies is
49,389,000 bb1.
2. Japan's stocks of petroleum on
December 31, 1941 are estimated at . . . 48,500,000 bbls.
plus such synthetic production and
produotion from shale as Japan may
have conserved during the period
1937-41. Synthetic and shale produc-
t1on 1s estimated at 1,500,000 bbls. a
year or 7,500,000 bbls. during the
period 1937-41.
MAR1
FILED
Japan's total reserve on
December 31, 1941 18, therefore,
estimated at
56,000,000 bb18.
3. It 1s
104

=== Page 283 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
3. It 1s estimated that this reserve is distributed
by produots as follows:
Aviation Gasoline*
20 1/2 months supply
Other Gasoline
31
Kerosene
18
Light & Heavy Fuels**
8
Lubricating O1le
20 1/2
n
*This 1s apparently a minimum estimate since
some of the gasoline classified as "other gasoline"
might be considered as aviation gasoline.If so
considered, the supply of aviation gasoline would
constitute a 31 months supply and the "other
gasoline" would be reduced to a 27 months supply.
**Light and heavy fuel oils are relatively
easy to produce and in the case of Tarakan,
Borneo, the crude oil 1s itself useable as fuel
oll without any refining. Consequently, Japan's
conquests in the Indies are likely to have
relieved the Japanese relative shortage of fuel
oils.
4. Total Netherlands East Indies
and British Borneo petroleum production
1n 1939 was
67,841,428 bb1s.
This quantity 1s more than enough to
satisfy Japan's total requirements and
consequently if capacity or near capacity
production can be restored by Japan within
a year or two and transported to points
of consumption, Japan's crude oil
requirements for a long war would be
amply

=== Page 284 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
amply met. This indicates the impor-
tance to Japan of drilling equipment
to restore the damaged wells of Borneo
and the Netherlands East Indies.
5.  Japan's annual refining capacity
is estimated at about
21,000,000 bb1s.
Consequently, Japan must increase her
refining oapacity to be able to fight a
prolonged war.
It 1s doubtful whether she can
extend her synthetic industry to any
great extent and, consequently,
possession of the refineries in the
Notherlands East Indies in operating
condition is of great importance to
Japan. In this connection it is
interesting to note that the Shell
refinery at Pladjoe near Palembang
had a oapac1ty of 40,000 bbls.of
crude per day or an annual capacity
of
14,600,000 bble.
and that the Standard-Vaouum refinery
at Soengi Gerong near Palembang had a
capacity of 43,000 bbls. of crude a
day

=== Page 285 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
day or an annual capacity of . . . . . . . 15,700,000 bbls.
Consequently, if Japan were able
(1) to use either or both of these
refineries at anything like their
former capacity and (2) to transport
their output freely, her position
for conducting a long war would be
greatly strengthened.
6. Japan's present fleet of high
speed tankers 1s apparently sufficient (even
taking into account delays due to proceeding
in oonvoys and probable losses) to trans-
port Japan's full requirements from the
Netherlands East Indies, provided the
Japanese navy 1s able to obtain the
bulk of its own petroleum supplies in
the Indies themselves. This again
emphasizes the special 1mportanoe to
Japan of the Netherlands East Indies
refinery equipment.

SEORET
JAPAN'S OIL TRANSPORT FJET
Between 1937 and 194l, Japan oonstruoted many fine tankers.
Suoh vessels as the NISSHO I AnU, the AIATSUKI MARU, nd the KENYO
MARU are exoellent examples of effioient tanker oonstruotion, be-
1ng ves els of 10,000 gross tons, 100,000-barrel capaoity and
20-knot speed. Without those new tankers, Japan's oil transport
problems after the outbreak of ar would have been serious indeed.
A large part of the balanoe of her fleet oonsisted of old vessels
of 12-knot speed and reduoed efficienoy.
On 7 December, 1941, Japan's tanker fleet oons1eted of 85
vessels, 564,216 gross tons. Inoluded 1n th1s figure were 77,280
tons of Navy oilers, ost of them old and slow, and obviously in-
udequate to serve all the necds of the fleet. Over 50 peroent of
Japan's requirements are naval. The Navy oilors are therefore
supplemented by fast merohant tankers; henoe any distinotion be-
tween the merohant tanker fleet and Navy oilers is theoretioal
and has little practioal significance.
Losses: Oil tankers huve been a major target of the Allied
offensive In the Paoifio. Recorded losses through January 31,
1944 stand at 43 vessels, 374,846 gross tons sunk. The net loss,
over construotion, for the whole pcriod since the beginning of the
war, amounts to z22,646 gross tons. In addition many vessels have
been demaged.
Aoguisitions and Construotion: The ocoupat1on of the Nether-
lands Indies solved Japen's supply problem, but trunsport was aro-
ther and fer more diffioult matter. As a result of the conquest
of the Indi es, Singapcre and othcr Allied possesslons, Japan eo-
quired 10 tankers of 37,196 tons. Th1s gave her a total of
601,412 gross tons capable or moving some 55,000,000 barrels cf
oil annually - an amount suffioicnt to mcet her current needs.
Japan's oonstruotion progranuc since Fearl Harbour 1s belleved
to have resulted 1n the addition of 19 vessels, 152,000 gross tons,
to her tanker fleet. reliminury estlmates for 1944 construction
are about 15 vessels of 125,000 tons. That Japan would 11ke to
build in exoess of this amouit ls olear but her ability to do so
is believed to be limited by oti.er demands on her shlpbuildine in-
dustry and the soaroity of muterlals and labour.
Because of these difrioulties, Japan must employ a oonsicerable
tonnage of dry oargo vessels to augment her tanker fleet. It 1s
believed that some of these vessels have been struoturally altered
for the oarriage of oils. Nany dry cargo vessels in Japan's rer-
ohant marine are eguipped w1th deep tanks taking up to 2,000
(12,0oo bbls.). These ships probably oarry the balanoe of their
cargo in drums of oil. This praotioe is not cntirely a matter of
expedienoy, sinoe drum shipments may sometimee be preferable, for
example in shipmonts to distant bases where bulk storage faoilities
are not available. In order to estimate the oil oarriage capaoity
of this olass of vessels, it has been caloulated that they oarry
seven times their gross tonnage in barrels. this estimate is ad-
mittedly on the low side. It nust be remembered that these ships 1
many oases way oarry only part of their ourgo in oil. More than
50 vessels of this type are now employed in oil transport.
Japan

=== Page 313 === (confidence 0.97)
- 2 -
Japan also employs barges for the sea transport of oil produots.
Self-propelled barges, as well as towed types, have been observed
in southern waters. There is some evidenoe that oil is being
transported by small wooden vessels, but this 1s not considered
to ocour in signifioant amounts at resent.
Current Situation and Prospects: Everything points to the
faot that the enemy'a ability to rove the required quantities of
oil has now reaohed a oritioal stage. Though his capaoity (in
barrels) is now higher thun it was at the start of the war, this
inorease has been aohieved only by less efficlent methods of
carriage and by depletion of the already hard pressed general
cargo fleet. Sinoe the rate of construction oan hardly be increased
it follows that if the rate of loss oontinues or inoreases, further
use must be made of the dry oargo fleet and other substitute nea-
sures. This in turn will weuken the enemy's ability to meet his
economio and military reguirenents.
